\section{Introduction}

\albertimportant{Interesting stuff to mention: 1) cost of BitZ do not depend on the ring, only on bit-size. 2) BitZ PCS can be used as drop-in replacement of any PCS?}


A Succinct Non-interactive Argument of Knowledge (SNARK) for a relation $\Relation$ is a non-interactive protocol that enables a prover to convince a verifier that, for a given public input $\inp$, they know a witness $\wit$ such that $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$, and where communication and verifier runtime are sublinear (i.e.\ the scheme is succinct).

In the vast majority of scenarios, $\Relation$ is defined over one (or more) algebraic object $R$, usually a finite field, and $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$ if and only if $(\inp,\wit)$ consists of elements that satisfy some polynomial equations. The choice of $R$ has a crucial impact on the design and end-to-end performance of the proof system, for two reasons: 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item
\label{i:arithmetization}
Usually the computations one wants to prove are not native to $R$, so one has to perform a more or less complex emulation of the computation into polynomial equations over $R$. This can affect the size of the input-witness pair $\inp,\wit$ significantly \cite{??}. Additionally, the emulation step can be  technically involved and prone to  security errors \cite{??}.  
\item
\label{i:field:performance}
The prover and verifier algorithms efficiency and design is affected by the choice of $R$: for example, modern CPUs are more performant for operations in certain small fields (e.g.\ M31 \cite{cryptoeprint:2023/824,CircleSTARKs}, Babybear \cite{risc0:whitepaper}, Goldilocks \cite{plonky2:repo}) than in arbitrary fields, especially if these are larger. Importantly, another class of fields where CPUs operate exceptionally fast are \emph{binary fields}, i.e.\ fields of order a power of two.
%
\end{enumerate}
\noindent Recently, two independent lines of research have received increased attention:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item Designing proof systems for relations expressed over $R=\ZZ$ the ring of integers, and over other rings that are not fields \cite{zaratan,zinc,zincplus,qaren,limber,more}. The motivation behind this is to address Item \ref{i:arithmetization} above: in many practical scenarios, the computation to be proved is most succinctly and easily expressed over such rings, see \cref{r:non_prime_characteristics} \albert{more refs?}. 
    \item Designing proof systems for $R=\comfield$ a binary field, for example \cite{stark,cryptoeprint:2022/1542,binius2,hashcaster:repo,flock}. The reason for this is twofold: first, some computations like classic hashes (SHA-256, Blake, etc.) are easier to express over  $\comfield$ than over other structures such as prime fields (however, as we discuss in \cref{?}, these hash functions remain relatively un-native to  $\comfield$ due to the hardness of emulating operations modulo $2^{32}$ or $2^{64}$). Second, binary fields are highly performant on modern CPU's, and further enable using a boon of protocol techniques that further benefit perfomance, most importantly \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes \cite{binius,binius2}. 
    %and sumcheck optimizations \cite{yuval,more?}.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Our contributions}
\albertimportant{It should be very clear throughout the paper: The PIOP machinery is for the most part Zinc+'s (which builds on Zinc and Zaratan). The dual field PCS primitive is novel; its instantiation is pretty much novel (though ideas can be traced back to \cite{binius64,lev}), and its enabling optimizations are novel. The PCS in binary fields is completely known. }

 In this work we bring these two lines of research together. We present \ftwoz, a hash-based proof system that: 
 \bigskip
 \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
     \item \ftwoz \ can prove polynomial constraints over essentially arbitrary rings $R$, including $\ZZ$, prime fields, lattices, binary fields, arbitrary field extensions, polynomial rings, etc., addressing Item \ref{i:arithmetization} above (\ftwoz\ can handle constraints over different rings at once).
     \item In its initial commitment step, \ftwoz's decomposes the witness $\wit$ into a vector of bits $\bitstext(\wit)$, and commits to $\bitstext(\wit)$ over a binary field $\comfield$ with an error correcting code (leveraging the packing technique from \cite{binius,binius2}).
     \item The initial polynomial constraints over the ring(s) $R$ are reduced to an MLE evaluation claim (or more generally, an inner product claim) over a finite field $\FF$, of the form $\langle \vv, \pi(\wit)\rangle = \mu\in \FF$, where $\pi$ is a projection from $R$ to $\FF$ and $\vv$ is a vector of elements from $\FF$.  The field $\FF$ can be a fixed field extension, a random prime field (following \cite{zaratan}), or a fixed prime field, depending on what  the ring(s) $R$ is (are).
     \item \ftwoz's Polynomial Commitment Scheme (PCS)  enables proving such linear claim efficiently. It operates, for the most part,    over $\comfield$. Thus, \ftwoz\ benefits of many of the advantages of running proof systems over binary fields (Item 2 discussed earlier one).   See below for a brief description of \ftwoz's PCS.
 \end{enumerate} 

 \noindent \ftwoz \ provides, among others, a compiler that takes any PCS over $\comfield$ and transforms it into a PCS over any of the above rings $R$, following the ``compiler'' frameworks of \cite{qaren,limber}. 


\bigskip


 \noindent We implement and open-source \ftwoz \ \cite{?}, and use it to prove a number of computations of practical interest. In \cref{t:intro_table_experiments} we provide a summary of the results. In all computations but one, \ftwoz \ outperforms the current state-of-the-art approaches, sometimes by orders of magnitudes (for example: ***). \ftwoz \ is even able to prove Babybear multiplications more efficiently than Plonky3, a mature proof system that operates natively on the Babybear field, and which is used in industry within a variety of projects \cite{?}.   \albert{Mention what is the security parameter}

We manage to instantiate our protocol using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$ in GHASH representation,  attain 128 bits of security, and have no limitation on the size of the ring $R$ we start with. See \cref{r:?,s:instantiation} for more comments and details on this. \albertimportant{For BabyBear bench, give separate numbers for PIOP part and the rest}

 \paragraph{\ftwoz's PCS in a nuthsell.}
%
 %\albertimportant{Ulrich's suggestion: First without tensor split, then explain the tensor split and why it is important}
%
 \ftwoz \ proves the aforementioned claim $\langle \vv, \pi(\wit)\rangle = \mu$ over a finite field $\FF$ as follows. We  describe first an initial, mostly insufficient, attempt.
     \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
         \item  \textbf{Bitification.} First, it transforms the  claim into a claim of the form  $\langle \uu, \pi(\bitstext(\wit))\rangle = \mu$ over $\FF$, for some $\uu$.
         \item \textbf{Lift to integers.} Assuming $\FF$ is a prime field of size $q$ and $\pi_q:\ZZ\to \FF$ is reduction modulo $|\FF|$, the prover sends $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle = \mu'$ in $\ZZ$.
         \item \textbf{Proof in the exponent of $\comfield$.} Now take a generator $g$ of the multiplicative subgroup $\comfield^*$ of $\comfield$, and prove that $g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle} = g^{\mu'}$ in $\comfield$. Concretely, we have
         \begin{equation}\label{e:intro}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle} = \prod_{i} \left((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\ww)_i + 1 \right) = g^{\mu'},
         \end{equation}
         which is a polynomial expression over $\comfield$ on the vector $\bitstext(\ww)$ that we committed over $\comfield$. 
         
         This is a standard situation where there is no ``wrong-field'' arithmetic, hence in principle one can use any proof system over $\comfield$ to prove \eqref{e:intro}.
     \end{enumerate}  
There are several issues with this attempt:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item First, $\FF$ may not be a prime field, in which case there is no natural lift to integers in Step 2; rather, there is a lift to integer polynomials $\ZZ[X]$. 
    \item Second, even if $\FF$ is a prime field,  $q=|\FF|$ may be large and the linear expression $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle$ may overflow the order of $g\in \comfield^{*}$, which is $2^{\comfieldexp}-1$. In this case, proving  $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle = \mu'$  ``in the exponent of $g$'' is unsound. 
    \item Third, even if neither of these problems hold, \eqref{e:intro} is, in principle, an expensive constraint to prove: indeed, it is a length $|\bitstext(\ww)|$ grand product of, essentially, arbitrary factors from $\comfield$ (typically $\approx$128 bit elements).  Grand products can be expensive to prove, and furthermore the fact that the factors are large prevents us from using known sumcheck optimizations such as univariate skips \cite{gruen} and \albert{what else?}.
    \item Four, when proving \eqref{e:intro}, typically one ends up with an MLE evaluation claim on the vector $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu)_i})_i$ (and another on $\bitstext(\ww)$, which is proved with any PCS over $\comfield$). If the verifier checks the claim itself, then it pays a linear cost. If the prover proves this claim, then it is essentially back to square one.
\end{itemize}

\bigskip

\noindent We solve these issues as follows: 1) if $\FF$ is a proper extension, at Step 2 we lift to a claim $\langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle = \mu' \in \ZZ[X]$ over $\ZZ[X]$,  where $\picanon:\ZZ[X] \to \FF$ is a canonical projection of $\ZZ[X]$ onto $\FF$. Then the verifier sends a random prime $q'$ of $\approx |\comfield^*|/|\bitstext(\ww)|$ size (small enough to avoid the issue of overflowing in the exponent), and a random element $\alpha\in \FF_{q'}$; then the previous claim is replaced by  $\langle \pi_{q',\alpha}\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu), \pi_{q',\alpha}(\bitstext(\wit))\rangle = \pi_{q',\alpha}(\mu') \in \FF_{q'}$ where $\pi_{q',\alpha}(f(X)) = \pi_{q'}(f)(\alpha)\in \FF_{q'}$. Now we re-enter Step 2 with this new claim.

2) If $\FF$ is too large of a prime field, we follow a similar approach as above: once we have the integer claim $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\wit)\rangle = \mu'$, the verifier samples a random ``small'' prime $q'$, reduce the claim modulo $q'$, and then re-enter Step 2 with this new claim.

\albert{remove?} We emphasize again that we manage to instantiate \ftwoz\ with 128 bits of security, using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, while having no limitation on the size of the ring $R$ we start with.

3) The main idea is to split  \eqref{e:intro} into $\approx\sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$ grand products of the same form as \eqref{e:intro}, so that, even though in total there are $|\bitstext(\ww)|$ factors among all of them, these factors can only take $\sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$ values. More concretely, we assume $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ tensor-decomposes into $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$ sized factors (this is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector), and then, assuming the notation of Step 3 above, we aim to prove  $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\wit)_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'$ in $\ZZ$  for all $j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$, such that $\sum_{j} \pi_q(\mu_j') \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu$, where we index   $\bitstext(\wit)$ with indices $i\in [|\uu^{(1)}|], j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$. We then set up a grand product for each of these claims, obtaining 
  \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_2}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\wit)_{*j}\rangle} = \prod_{i} (g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij} + 1 = g^{\mu_j'} \qquad \text{for all } j.
         \end{equation}
There are $|\bitstext(\wit)|$ factors in total, but only $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\wit)|}$ different values these factors can take. 

We say \eqref{e:intro_2} is a \emph{low entropy batched grand product}. To prove \eqref{e:intro_2} we use a batched GKR protocol with the following  optimization, among others: because of the low-entropy phenomenon, we build several small lookup tables of size $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|})$ (of a few MB at most) which are used during the most expensive sumcheck rounds of the GKR protocol. Concretely, the last sumcheck (the most expensive one), uses $3$ lookup tables to compute the first three messages of the prover; the second last sumcheck does the same for the first two prover messages (with two tables); and the third last sumcheck does the same for the first prover message (with one table).


The above solution also takes care of Issue 4: the batched GKR protocol outputs MLE evaluation claims on $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})_i})_i$ (and $\bitstext(\ww)$, which are dealt with the PCS over $\comfield$). This is an $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|})$ vector, and the verifier can check this claim by itself cheaply.



    % \begin{enumerate}         
      %   \item  Then, if necessary (if $\FF$ is not a prime field or if $\FF$ is too large), it replaces this claim by a new claim over a small prime field $\FF'$ (with $|\FF'|\lessapprox |\comfield|/\sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$). 
      %   \item Then it splits the current claim into $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$  linear claims on chunks of $\bitstext(\ww)$ (leveraging tensor decomposability of $\uu$). 
      %   \item Then, finally, it proves all the resulting claims in the exponent of a generator of $\comfield^*$, via a batched GKR grand product argument.

       %  The factors of these batched grand products turn out to have ``low-entropy'' (meaning that there are $|\bitstext(\ww)|$ factors but only   $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\ww)|}$ possible values among them. We design specific optimizations that leverage such phenomenon.

    % \end{enumerate}

 \bigskip 
 
 \paragraph{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete dually linear relations, and arithmetization of SHA-256} Observe that, once we have a standard linear claim on $\bitstext(\ww)$ in $\comfield$, we could replace  $\bitstext(\ww)$ by a linear expression on some other committed vector $\ff$ over $\comfield$, so that $\bitstext(\ww) = M \cdot \ff$ for some matrix $M$. The final linear claim can then be replaced by a linear claim for $\ff$. Hence, we can, instead of committing to $\bitstext(\ww)$, commit to $\bff$ and treat $\ww$ and $\bitstext(\ww)$ as virtual vectors. See \cref{?} for details. 
 
 This allows \ftwoz\  to prove polynomial constraints over $R$ on vectors $\ww$ that are a virtual $\FF_2$-linear combination of a committed vector $\ff\in \comfield^{\codedim'}$. For example, \ftwoz \ can efficiently prove the following R1CS-like relation: 
%
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ & \qquad\qquad \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \ww) \circ (B\cdot \ww) = C \cdot \ww\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\zz, \bff)\right)   \end{aligned} \right.\right\},
\end{align*}
%
where $\pi_2$ denotes reduction modulo $2$. A particularly interesting subclass of this relation is that obtained by setting $A=B=\mathbf{0}$, in which case the resulting relation, which we call the \emph{CM relation}, degenerates to linear constraints over $\ZZ$ and over $\FF_2$, i.e.\ a ``dually linear'' constraint. 

In \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete} we show that the CM relation is NP-complete \albert{Or NP-hard?}. In \cref{?} we arithmetize SHA-256 as an instance of the CM relation, with *** smaller witnesses than Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} \albertimportant{add numbers. Mention that we don't prove hashes faster though (but we have better proof size and memory so...)}

\subsection{Related work}

\paragraph{Proof systems over integers and other rings.} In \cite{zaratan}, Campanelli and Hall-Andersen propose proving polynomial constraints over integers, by projecting them onto a random prime field (sampled by the verifier), a technique which the authors call \emph{fingerprinting}. Subsequent works have adopted this idea \cite{zinc,qaren,zincplus,limber}. \cite{zaratan} also proposes a hidden-order group based PCS suitable for their PIOP, similar to the ones in \cite{DARK,BlockDARK}. The PCS is not implemented and so we do not compare against it.

The Zinc \cite{zinc} work adopts \emph{fingerprinting} and designs a hash-based PCS over the rationals $\QQ$, suitable for the fingerprinting approach, providing the first relatively practical proof system over the integers. \cite{zinc} addresses subtle technical issues that arise when aiming to commit to \emph{integer} vectors with error correcting codes (namely, malicious provers can potentially use rational numbers that are very close to being integers of the target magnitude).

The Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} work extends \cite{zinc} in two directions: 1) It proposes a constraint system that includes polynomial constraints simultaneously over many types of rings\footnote{In the terminology of \cite{zinc+}, the constraints are over \emph{polynomial rings with ideal membership constraints}, for certain types of ideals (generated by a monic and non-constant polynomial, or zero). However this is equivalent to saying the constraints are over different types of rings (within a certain class)} and fields, and provides a method for constructing PIOP's for those. This method extends the fingerprinting approach by not only projecting $\ZZ$ onto a random prime field, but projecting all sorts of rings and fields onto a random prime field. 2) It improves the soundness analysis of the PCS in \cite{zinc}, plus it proposes a new linear code over $\ZZ$ or $\QQ$, called Integer Pseudo Reed-Solomon (IPRS) code, obtained by ``lifting'' (in a suitable way) an FFT encoding algorithm over a small fintie field, to the integers. \albert{compare \ftwoz \  to Zinc+?}



In \cite{qaren} the authors propose $\QQ$aren, a compiler that takes any PCS over a finite field of sufficently large characteristic and creates a PCS for integer polynomials (or, more precisely, rational polynomials close to the target integer polynomials, see the overview of \cite{zinc} above). The authors instantiate their compiler to obtain the first transparent and fully succinct PCS over integers/rationals. The prior work \cite{zinc,zincplus} is not succinct on the bit-size of the integers being committed. No implementation or performance reports is preovided in \cite{qaren}.

The concurrent work Limber \cite{limber} proposes a new compiler that transforms any PCS over fields of sufficiently large characteristic $q$ (in this case, $q$ is $\Omega(\lambda^2 \dot \log(N))$, $N$ being the witness size, while in \cite{qaren} it is $\Omega(2^\lambda)$). Additionally Limber compiles into strictly integer PCS, and does not present the issue that a malicious prover could use rationals that are ``almost integers''. Limber is instantiated in practice with the Hyrax PCS (in contrast to \cite{zinc,zincplus} which use a hash based PCS).  \albertimportant{Wait for the implementation to come out so we can discuss its performance against prior work better}. In \cite{?} we compare \ftwoz \ against Limber, obtaining  *** \albertimportant{fill in}.

\paragraph{Proof systems over binary fields.} Hash-based SNARKs over binary fields were pioneered by the line of work of Ben-Sasson et al.~\cite{FRI,stark,linearSizedIOPs,cryptoeprint:2022/1542}, but did not see widespread adoption until the introduction of \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes by Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius,binius2}; see also \cite{binius64}. Such schemes allow one to commit to a bit-valued witness over an extension field without incurring embedding overhead, while still providing evaluation proofs for its original ``uncompressed'' representation. The \emph{ring-switching} technique of Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius2} is generic, enabling the use of codes over larger alphabets without introducing representational redundancy.\footnotemark 
A particularly attractive choice is Reed--Solomon codes over $GF(2^k)$ \cite{binius64,hashcaster:repo}, which offer an excellent trade-off between distance and encoding cost, although several works~\cite{Blaze,tensorswitch,Bolt} explore this design space using more efficient codes.
%the binary and packed version of the Basefold PCS.   
\footnotetext{%
Alternative approaches based on Galois conjugates have been proposed thereafter~\cite{lev:hashcaster,FRIBinius:Galois}.
Remarkably, the Galois approach is present in prior work \cite[Appendix F]{stark}, although not explicitly framed in full generality.
}

At the time of writing, two further works push the performance envelope of binary proof systems. Bünz, Rothblum, and Wang~\cite{flock} combine \cite{binius64} with the batched R1CS strategy inspired by~\cite{linearSizedIOPs}, achieving a striking proving throughput of several hundred thousand hashes per second on an M4 Max CPU. BinarySpartan~\cite{binaryspartan}, in turn, incorporates the optimizations of \cite{binius64}-Flock into Spartan, resulting in a proof system with again high hash-proving throughput.
% \ulrich{Not sure about Flock and Binary Spartan, since nothing conceptually new is happening there.
% The main improvement over binius64 stems from batching R1CS instances as in \cite{linearSizedIOPs}, and to be frank I do not know what Spartan is about, since binius64 and Flock are essentially Spartan.
% I have the impression that naming them requires also to provide a more complete list of binary provers.
% }

\paragraph{Commitments in binary fields with openings in rings $R$ of characteristic different than $2$.} To our knowledge this primitive is studied only in Soukhanov's note \cite{lev} and in the present work. The note \cite{lev}  proposes a scheme for committing over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$ and then executing a PCS opening over $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (a prime field), by running the PCS in the exponent of a generator of   $\FF_{2^{127}}^*$. The approach, substantially different to ours, is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (or analogous configurations where $2^n-1$ is prime) and does not seem to be able to leverage our aforementioned ``low-entropy'' based optimiziations. The note does not provide an implementation nor performance reports. We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}.

 $\QQ$aren and Limber \cite{qaren,limber} provide a related primitive when $R=\ZZ$, but can only support commitments over fields of relatively large characteristic (compared to $2$).

\subsection{A simple version of \ftwoz}\label{s:to_simple_bitz}

We explain how \ftwoz \ works. Throughout the exposition we make several simplifying assumptions, for the sake of clarity. Then we address the general cases in \cref{s:to_general_bitz}.

Let  $R$ be a ring, for example $R$ may be the integers $\ZZ$, a prime field, a lattice ring, a finite field extension, etc. For simplicity, for now let $R$ be either $\ZZ$ or a finite field $\KK$ (possibly an extension field). In \cref{s:to_general_bitz} we treat the general case. If $R=\ZZ$, then we assume a public parameter $B$ specifying the maximum bit-size of a witness element.

In \ftwoz, given a public vector $\inp=\xx\in R^\inplen$, the prover wants to prove it knows $\wit=\ww\in R^n$ such that \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_0}\pp_i(\xx,\ww)=0 \qquad \text{in  } R \qquad \text{ for all } i\in [m], \end{equation} where $\pp=(\pp_i)_{i\in [m]}$ is a collection of polynomials over $R$.  For example, in an \emph{R1CS constraint}, $$\pp_i(\xx,\ww) = (A\cdot (\xx,\ww))_i \cdot (B\cdot (\xx,\ww))_i  - (C\cdot (\xx,\ww))_i$$
where $A,B,C$ are $m\times (\inplen + n)$ matrices over $R$. 

%\bigskip

%\noindent \ftwoz \ proceeds as follows:

 \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment in $\comfield$.} The prover creates a vector $\bitstext(\ww) \in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}$ of integer bits, according to some public specification of how to represent elements  $r\in R$ as a linear combination of  bits, i.e.\ $r=L(\bitstext(r))$ for $L: \{0,1\}^{\codedim/n}\to R$ a linear map.  For example, if $R=\ZZ$, then $L(b_1,\ldots, b_{B}) = \sum_{i\in [B]} 2^{i-1} \cdot b_i$.

     Then the prover looks at $\bitstext(\ww)$ as a vector from $\FF_2^{\codedim}$, encodes it with a Reed-Solomon code (or any other code) over $\comfield$, and sends a Merkle root of the resulting codeword to the verifier. 

     When encoding $\bitstext(\ww)$, every $\comfieldexp$ bits $b_1, \ldots, b_\comfieldexp$ are packed as a single field element $\sum_{i\in [\comfield]} X^{i-1}\cdot b_i$ from $\comfield$, following \cite{binius} (one can use a different $\FF_2$-base than the monomial base $(X^i)_i$).
     
     \albertimportant{We may want to mention that we do an OOD sample if we are working beyond the UDR}
     
     \paragraph{Step 2: Projection to a   finite field $\FF$.}  \albertimportant{} If necessary, the verifier samples a random map $\pi$ from $R$ to a finite field $\FF$ of $\approx \lambda$ bit size (not necessarily a prime field). Then the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_0} is replaced by
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_05}\pi(\pp_i)(\pi(\xx),\pi(\ww))=0 \qquad \text{in  } \FF \qquad \text{ for all } i.\end{equation} 
The form of the samplable maps $\pi$ depends on the type of ring $R$ we started with. For example, if $R=\ZZ$, then $\pi$ is reduction modulo a random prime $q$, following \cite{zaratan}. If $R=\KK$ is a finite field (not necessarily a prime field), then $\pi$ is always the identity. In \cref{s:to_general_bitz} we discuss other rings and other projections.

This step can be skipped if one already has an efficient PIOP for polynomial constraints over $R$; for example, if $R$ is a finite field of reasonable size. 

If $R$ is a finite field of very large size, one may prefer to lift the initial polynomial constraints to $\ZZ$  first (or $\ZZ[X]$ if $R$ is field extension), and then project onto a random prime field $\FF$ of bit-size $\approx \lambda$ during this step. This can compress the bit-size of the witness in the next steps by a very large factor.
     \paragraph{Step 3: PIOP over $\FF$.} As in \cite{zaratan,zincplus,limber}, a PIOP is run over $\FF$, reducing \eqref{e:intro_eq_05} to a linear claim over $\FF$ or a field extension  $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)}$ of $\FF$. The claim is  of the form
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_1}
\langle \vv, \pi(\ww)\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in } \FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)},
\end{equation}
for some $\mu\in\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)}$, $\vv\in (\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)})^\codedim$. 

In general, if $\FF$ is a prime field of $\approx \lambda$ bits (e.g.\ if we started with $R=\ZZ$), then $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}=\FF$. The field $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}$ may be a proper extension if, for example, we started with $\RR=\KK$ a small finite field (in which case $\pi=\mathsf{Id}$ and $\KK=\FF$),  and the PIOP had to use challenges in an extension of $\FF$ to guarantee soundness.
 
We  assume $\vv$ admits a tensor decomposition $\vv=\vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} \in \FF_q^{n_1 \cdot n_2}$. This is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector. This will be leveraged crucially, for efficiency purposes, at the later Step 5.2.

For simplicity, we assume $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}=\FF$ is a prime field. We discuss the general case in \cref{s:to_general_bitz}. 



\paragraph{Step 4: Bitification of the linear claim.} The claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_1} is replaced by \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_2}\langle \uu, \pi(\bitstext(\ww))\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF,
\end{equation} 
for a new $\uu\in \FF^{\codedim}$. This transformation is done using the linear bit representation $L$ mentioned in Step 1. Concretely, representing $L$ as matrix multiplication and indexing vectors appropriately, $\langle \vv, \pi(\ww)\rangle = \langle \vv, \pi( L \cdot \bitstext(\ww)) \rangle = \langle \pi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv, \pi(\bitstext(\ww)) \rangle$.

The new vector $\uu$ still admits tensor decompositions $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with, say $\uu^{(1)}\in \FF^{\codedim_1}$ and $\uu^{(2)}\in \FF^{\codedim_2}$. Indeed, one can see that $L= \mathsf{Id}_{n\times n} \otimes L'$ for some vector $L'\in R^{\codedim/n}$ and $\mathsf{Id}$ the identity matrix of dimension $n$. Then for example $\uu^{(1)} = \vv^{(1)}$ and $\uu^{(2)} = \vv^{(2)} \otimes \pi(L')$, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} for details \albert{Review. Maybe I could prove it here}. Other choices of decompositions may lead to better efficiency later.  % assume for the sake of efficiency later on that $\uu$ also admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu_1\otimes \uu_2 \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2}$. This is in general a free assumption, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} \albert{add a remark not in the main body?}.
 
 We assume that  $|\FF|<  \fieldboundsizeexponent$, a condition needed for soundness later in Step 5.2. Below in in \cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS} we explain   how to handle larger $\FF$. We note that this is a very constraining assumption because, when for example aiming to use $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$ (a highly performant binary field) and 128 bits of security, one can easily have $\FF$ with $|\FF|\geq  \fieldboundsizeexponent$.


     \paragraph{Step 5: \ftwoz \ Polynomial Commitment Scheme (PCS).} Our \ftwoz\  PCS  enables efficiently proving  \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} with $\bitstext(\ww)$ being committed over $\comfield$ (in packed form). This is done as follows:


     \paragraph{Step 5.0: reducing the prime modulus if necessary.} This step triggers if the condition  $|\FF|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ is not met. We explain this step below in \cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS} and assume for now that the condition holds. 
     
     When $\FFext$ is a proper field extension, this step always triggers.

     \paragraph{Step 5.1: Transformation of \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} into a batch of low-magnitude integer claim.} The prover organizes $\bitstext(\ww)$ into a $\codedim_1\times \codedim_2$ matrix with columns $\bitstext(\ww)_{*j}\in \bits^{\codedim_1}$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$). Then it publishes   $\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_{\codedim_2}\in  \ZZ$ defined as 
         $\eta_j=\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\ww)_{*j}\rangle$  in $\ZZ$, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$.  
         Then the verifier checks that \albert{Add a remark about the possibility of proving the below with a sumcheck}
         %
         $$
        \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi(\eta_j) \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF,
         $$
         and \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} is replaced by the integer claim \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_3}\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\ww)_{*j}\rangle =\eta_j \qquad \text{in  } \ZZ \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
        \end{equation} 
        where $\pi^{-1}$ is the canonical representation of elements from $\FF$ as integers in the range $\range{0}{|\FF|-1}$.
        \paragraph{Step 5.2: Exponentiation in $\comfield$ and grand product in $\comfield$.}  Since we assumed $|\FF|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, \eqref{e:intro_eq_3} is true if and only if
          \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4}
          \begin{aligned}
          g^{\eta_j}  &= g^{\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\ww)_{*j}\rangle}\\ &= g^{\sum_{i\in[\codedim_1]} \pi^{-1}(\uu_i) \cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij} } = \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} g^{ \pi^{-1}(\uu_i) \cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij} } 
          \\ &= \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij}  +1\right) \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
          \end{aligned}
        \end{equation} 
        where $g$ is a generator of the multiplicative subgroup of $\comfield$, and in the middle expression, $\bitstext(\ww)_{ij}$ denotes the $i,j$-th entry (a bit) in $\bitstext(\ww)$ seen as an element of $\comfield$.

        The prover and verifier run  a batched grand product PIOP over the binary field $\comfield$ for the claim  \eqref{e:intro_eq_4}, obtaining as a result a new claim of the form
        \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5} \langle \zz, \bitstext(\ww)_{*j} \rangle = c_j \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2],
        \end{equation} 
        for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}$ and $c_j\in \comfield$. In general, these claims can be batched to \begin{equation} \label{e:intro_eq_5_batched}\langle \zz', \bitstext(\ww) \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield\end{equation} for  $\zz'\in \comfield^{\codedim}, c\in \comfield$ with, for example, $\zz'=(\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}) \otimes \zz$ for $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}$ verifier's randomness, and $c=\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \alpha_j\cdot c_j$. 
        However, in most instantiations this step may not be necessary, especially if using an interleaved code to commit to $\bitstext(\ww)$ \albert{In the GKR grand product section of the main body I may need to mention batching?}. 

        This exponentiation trick is inspired by  \cite{binius64,lev}, where the same idea is used in  different contexts, see \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}. 



        This is by far the most expensive step of \ftwoz. Our grand product PIOP is a simple GKR protocol for grand products, for which we design novel \albert{to our knowledge} prover optimizations, see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr} below and \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. These optimizations exploit the ``low entropy'' of the factors $(g^{\pi^{-1}}-1)\cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij} +1$ across $(i,j)\in [\codedim_1]\times [\codedim_2]$, in the sense that across $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ factors, there are only $\codedim_1+1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ possible values taken by these factors.  This is why it is important to have the vectors $\vv$ and $\uu$ admit a tensor split into $\approx$square-root sized factors. % See \cref{?,s:gkr_low_entropy} for more details.


  
 \paragraph{Step 5.3: Proving linear claims over $\comfield$.} At this point, since $\bitstext(\ww)$ was committed over $\comfield$ (in packed form), one can prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} (or the batched version of it) via standard methods such as in \cite{binius,binius2,flock}. Concretely, we use the so-called ring switch from \cite{binius,binius2} to turn \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} into linear claims about the packed binary field elements we encoded, and then use WHIR \cite{whir} over $\comfield$ to prove these claims. See \cref{a:ring_switch_1,a:ring_switch_remco} for details on ring-switching. 


\bigskip       

 
    In \cref{s:instantiation} we discuss how to efficiently instantiate \ftwoz \ with $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, and attain 128 bits of security.




\begin{remark}[Description of Step 5.0]\label{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}
\albertimportant{In the actual protocol this step is done after Step 5.1. I think they are equivalent but should unify}
    Assume that $\FFext=\FF$ is a prime field (the general case is described in \cref{s:to_general_bitz}), and that in Step 5.0 the condition $|\FF|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ does not hold. Then the prover sends an integer $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that
    %
    $$\langle  \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bitstext(\ww)\rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ,$$    
    The verifier checks that $\pi(\mu')=\mu$, and responds with a random prime $q$ such that $q< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ Then prover and verifier proceed to Step 5.1 after updating $\FF \gets \FF_{q'}$ the prime field with $q'$ elements, $\pi\gets \pi_{q'}$, $\mu \gets \pi_q(\mu')$, and $\uu\gets \pi_q\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pi_q\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)})$.
\end{remark}



\begin{remark}[Other usages of the ``prove-in-the-exponent'' approach]
       There are other projects (e.g.\ Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Shoukhanov's note \cite{lev}) that use exponentiation to prove integer (or mod $|\comfield|-1$) claims in binary fields, none of these (to our knowledge) is used or is usable in a context that results in the low entropy grand products of ours, where our optimizations apply.  We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}
      \end{remark}  

 \subsubsection{Intuition of soundness proofs}

 We prove that the above protocol (and the more general version in \cref{s:to_general_bitz}) is round-by-round knowledge sound. We next provide the main ideas of why this is the case.

 \albert{todo}
 
\subsection{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms in \ftwoz \ and NP-complete dually linear relations}\label{s:to_f2_virtual}



Observe that, at the end of the day, Step 5.2 outputs a linear claim of the form\footnote{We start with the batched version of the claim, but the ideas in this section can be adapted to \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} as well.}
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_repeated} \langle \zz, \bitstext(\ww) \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield,
\end{equation} 
for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and $c\in \comfield$. This is a standard linear claim over $\comfield$ for the vector $\bitstext(\ww)$ which we committed to previously. 


Now, say that we know that $\bitstext(\ww)$ is a linear transformation of a vector $\bff\in  \FF_2^{\codedim'} \subseteq  \comfield^{\codedim'}$, so that
%
$$
\bitstext(\ww) = M \cdot \bff \qquad \text{ in } \comfield,
$$
for some matrix $M\in \FF_2^{\codedim\times \codedim'}$.
Then 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}
 \langle \zz, \bitstext(\ww) \rangle  =  \langle M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \zz, \bff \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield.
\end{equation}
Hence, in this scenario, one can commit to $\bff$ instead of $\bitstext(\ww)$ (again, in packed formed if necessary),  replace the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}, and prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_virtual} in Step 5.3 of \ftwoz. 



Thus, \ftwoz\ can be expanded so as to prove claims where we are given a public vector $\inp=\xx\in R^\inplen$, and the prover claims  it knows $\wit=\bff\in \comfield^{\codedim'}$ such that 

\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_0_virtual}
\begin{cases}
&\pp_i(\xx,\ww)=0 \qquad \text{in  } R \qquad \text{ for all } i\in [m], \\ &\bitstext(\ww)=\pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
%
where  $\pp=(\pp_i)_{i\in [m]}$ is a collection of polynomials over $R$; and $\pi_2$ is reduction modulo $2$ \albert{I may have been loose about when $\bitstext(\ww)$ is in $\FF_2$ and when in integers during the technical overview, review}.  

A particularly interesting instantiation of this relation is the following R1CS-like relation over the integers, where $\ww$ is a vector of bits (hence $\ww=\bitstext(\ww)$) and $\ww= \pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)$:


    
%
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ &\qquad \qquad\qquad \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \ww) \circ (B\cdot \ww) = C \cdot \ww\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\zz, \bff)\right)   \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
\end{align*}


An interesting subset  of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$
 is that where $A$ and $B$ are zero matrices. We call the resulting relation the \emph{CM} relation because of the matrices $C$ and $M$:
 $$
\Relation_{\mathsf{CM}}(\ZZ) \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times \ZZ^{m\times \codedim} \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&C \cdot \ww = 0\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\zz, \bff)\right) \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
$$
%
In a sense, the CM relation places only linear constraints over its witness (with some constraints happening in $\comfield$ and other in $\ZZ$). Yet, the CM relation is NP-complete, as shown below in \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete}. 


Indeed, in \cref{s:sha256-f2-arithmetization} \textbf{we arithmetize SHA-256 as a CM instance}. Our arithmetization leads to witnesses  of size (in bits) **** \albertimportant{report comparison with Flock/Binius in witness size. Mention as well the performance comparison}.

\begin{remark}[The $\RELCM$ relation is NP-complete]\label{r:CM_is_NP_complete}
Any Boolean circuit can be arithmetized as an instance of $\RELCM$. Indeed, since any boolean circuit can be written as a system of equations using the operations XOR and AND, it suffices to prove that, given $x,y,z\in \bits$, we can express both of the following constraints as an instance of $\RELCM$:
%
$$    x \xor y = z,\qquad \qquad 
    x \band y = z.
$$
Indeed, $x \xor y = z$ if and only if $\pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y))=z$, where here $+$ means addition modulo $2$. Further, $x \band y = z$  if and only if  
%
$$
2\cdot z =  x+y - \pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y)).
$$    
One can verify this last equivalence for example  by directly inspecting that it holds for the four possible combinations of $x,y\in \bits$.
\end{remark}

%\albertimportant{Not sure this is needed depending on how we write the technical overview.} 

%\albertimportant{Maybe we should have general R1CS here, not just linear R1CS. And then describe the CM relation a subset of the more general R1CS relation}

%
%    $\RELCM$ is related to PIMSAT and $\LinBitsDual^{\mathsf{virtual}}$ in the sense that $\RELCM$ is the result of taking  $\PIMSAT(\bits, \pp_{\mathsf{CM}}, \bot)$ (i.e.\ the PIMSAT relation with $R=\ZZ, \moduledegree=0, B=1$ and no ideals) and replacing the vector input-witness vector in PIMSAT by a vector of the form $\mathbf{h}= \pi_2^{-1}\left(\Mmatrix\cdot \pi_2(\bff)\right)$.
    %
%\end{remark}

\subsection{GKR for low-entropy batched grand products}\label{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}
   Recall that, in Step 5.2 of \ftwoz \ (\cref{s:to_simple_bitz}) the prover must prove that
          \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4_repeated}g^{\langle \pi^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\ww)_{*j}\rangle} = \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij}  +1 =g^{\eta_j} \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
        \end{equation} 
        %
        For efficiency purposes, we assume  both $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$ are $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ (see \cref{s:instantiation} for exact parameter instantiations).  At a protocol level, we simply prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_4_repeated} with a standard GKR protocol for grand products \cite{thaler?}. Concretely, let $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}$ and $\codedim_2=2^{r_2}$. We set up multilinear polynomials $L_{0}, L_1,\ldots, L_{r_1}$ on $r_2 + i$ variables for $i\in \range{0}{r_1}$, respectively, such that 
        %
        $$
        L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = \left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1\right)\cdot \bitstext(\ww)_{ij}  +1  \quad  \text{ for all } (\bb,\cc) \in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2},
        $$
        and, for $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$
      \begin{equation}\label{e:to_gkr_polys}
  L_{i}(\bb,\cc)  =  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1, \cc)  \quad \text{for all } \bb\in \bits^{i} \times \bits^{r_2}.
    \end{equation}
    We then use a series of sumchecks to certify \cref{e:to_gkr_polys} for all $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$, and $L_{0}(\cc) = g^{\eta_{\mathsf{int}(\cc)}}$ for all $\cc\in \bits^{r_2}$.  
    
    We emphasize again that, at a protocol level, this GKR scheme is completely standard. See \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy} for all the details. 
    
    
    At the end of the GKR grand product, the prover and verifier are left with MLE evaluation claims of the form:
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1}
    \mle{g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu)}}(\rr) = a,\qquad \mle{\bitstext(\ww)_{*j}}(\rr)=a_j \qquad \text{for all } j \in [\codedim_2]
    \end{equation}
    for some $\rr\in \comfield^{r_1}$, where $\mle{*}$ denotes MLE extension, and $g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu)}$ is the vector with entries $g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu_i)}$. 
    
    Since $\uu$ is public, the verifiers checks the first claim of \eqref{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1} on its own in time $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$. This is one, among several, reasons why the tensor split of $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ and the splitting the original claim into a batch of $\codedim_2$ claims (see Step 5.1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}) pays off. 


    
    We design novel (to our knowledge \albert{check}) prover optimizations for the GKR grand product (without altering the protocol itself), see \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for details. Namely, we exploit the following property, which is enabled, again, by the tensor split of $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ and the splitting the original claim into a batch of $\codedim_2$ claims:
    \medskip

    \begin{quote}\emph{\textbf{Low entropy grand products:}} There are at most $\codedim_1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ values that $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)$ can take on the hypercube of size $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$. \end{quote}

     

    This property trickles down to different stages of the GKR protocol. For example, the above property implies that $L_{r_1-1}(\bb',\cc)$ as well can take only an $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ amount of values (with worse constants now) across all $(\bb',\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}$. Similarly, the coefficients of the first messages sent by the prover at the last stages of the GKR protocol (which involve the most expensive sumchecks), also benefit from a low entropy property. 

    This allows the  prover to compute small lookup tables that are kept on cache and reused across all values of $\cc\in [\codedim_2]$. We build different lookup tables used at different stages of the GKR protocol.  For example, we use 4 lookup tables in the first round of the last sumcheck of GKR (the most expensive sumcheck, being on $r_1+r_2$ variables). These tables are used to compute the first prover message of such sumcheck. We then construct 3 more tables to compute the second's prover message, and finally we use one last table to compute the third prover message. Similarly, we build lookup tables for the first rounds of the second and third last sumchecks of the GKR protocol.

\begin{comment}
    \paragraph{Comparison with the grand products of \cref{lev,binius64}} \albertimportant{do we really need this part if we already have \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}}

    Binius64 \cite{?}, a proof system for polynomial constraints over $\comfield$,  multiplication of integers modulo $m=|\comfield|-1$, say $x\cdot y = z \mod m$,  by proving $g^{x\cdot y} = g^z$. More concretely, given vectors $\ww_1,\ww_2,\ww_3\in \ZZ^n$, Binius64 proves $\ww_{3i}= \ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i} \mod m$ for all $i\in [n]$ by proving \begin{equation}\label{e:to_binius_product}g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{\ww_{3i}} \qquad \text{for all } i\in [n].\end{equation} Using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, Binius64  is then able to prove \emph{integer} multiplication (without modular reduction) of $64$-bit integers, by mixing in a simple small constraint over $\comfield$. 

    Intuitively, the main qualitative difference between Binius64 and our setting is that the former has elements of the form ``witness $\cdot$ witness'' in the exponent of $g$. On the other hand, our exponents contain linear combinations of elements of the form ``(public value) $\cdot$ witness``, and the public values split into a tensor product. This ultimately allows us to create $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ grand products where the constant elements $g^{\uu_i}$ are publicly known and the same across all $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ grand products, yielding the  low entropy property that our optimizations exploit. 
    
    In the Binius64 setting, on the other hand, this split is not available because the witness vector, in general, does not admit a tensor split \albert{ is it only because of that? or are there more reasons?}.

\subsubsection{Comparison to other ``prove-in-the-exponent'' usages}\label{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}


\albert{Missing refs? Longfellow?}

   
        \paragraph{Binius64} For example, \cite{binius64}  multiplies pairs of 64-bit integer witness elements in the exponent of $g$. This is substantially different from our setting where, when binary fields come into play, we are only interested in proving an integer claim about a linear combination of elements of the form ``(public values)$\cdot$ (witness elements)'', i.e.\ $\langle \vv, \ww\rangle = \mu$ in $\ZZ$, with the public values $\vv$ admitting a tensor split $\vv=\vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}$ (also \ftwoz \ can prove multiplication of all sorts of ring elements, not just 64-bit integers).   
        This simpler setting is what allows us to use the low entropy optimizations designed in this work.  Crucially, \textbf{the reason why we only need to address this setting is because we start off with constraints and a PIOP over integers/fields/rings (Steps 2 to 4 inclusive), and only ever move to work on binary fields when we need to prove an MLE evaluation of the witness (a linear claim) over a ``small'' prime field (Step 5)}. 

        
        
        %The tensor split allows us to replace the claim into $\codedim_2=|\vv^{(2)}|$ claims $\langle \vv^{(1)}, \ww_{*j}\rangle = \eta_j$ in $\ZZ$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$. These claims are then each proved ``in the exponent''. Since $\vv^{(1)}$ is common across all claims, and $\ww$ is filled with bits, the resulting grand products are low entropy. 
        
      
        \cite{binius64} on the other hand multiplies 64-bit integer witness in the exponent directly. More precisely, \cite{binius64} proves the identity $g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{2^{64}\cdot \ww_{3i}} \cdot g^{\ww_{4i}}$ for $\ww_1, \ldots, \ww_4$ vectors of 64-bit integers (together with a small additional constraint to address possible overflow). This is done through a combination of LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, a GKR grand product (for relatively small products), and the Frobenius automorphism trick. It does not seem possible to follow a similar optimization strategy to ours.
       

        We  emphasize again that \ftwoz \ supports proving operations of all kinds of ring elements (small and large integers, field elements, lattice ring elements, extension field elements), as opposed to \cref{binius64} where multiplication is limited to 64-bit integers.

        In \cref{s:exp_binius64_ip} we compare the performance of \ftwoz \ and Binius64 when multiplying integers. \ftwoz \ outperforms Binius64 by \albert{fill in}. 


        \paragraph{The note \cite{lev}.} In the note \cite{lev}, Soukhanov proposes a scheme for committing to a vector of bits $\ww$ over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$, and proving MLE evaluation claims over the prime field  $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, for a vector of elements from   $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ obtained by packing bits from $\ww$ together. To do so, at a high level, the MLE evaluation claims are proved ``in the exponent'' in the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$, leveraging that  the multiplicaitve subgroup of  $\FF_{2^{127}}$ has prime order $|\FF_{2^{127}}^*| = 2^{127}-1$. The proposed approach is substantially different from ours: \cite{lev} essentially takes a polylogarithmic PCS over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, such as Basefold \cite{basefold}, WHIR \cite{whir}, etc.,  and executes it ``in the exponent of $\FF_{2^{127}}$''. To do so, it relies on the lookup argument LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, grand product arguments (for small products), the Frobenious trick, and additional commitments, for every of the $\log\ell$ rounds.  
        
        In its execution,  \cite{lev} and Binius64 \cite{binius64} seem to us to be more similar than any of these two to \ftwoz. Indeed, they both use, mostly, the same collection of subprotocols, in a similar manner. Because of this we expect both approaches to have comparable performance profiles, though we do not validate this hypothesis due to \cite{lev} not reporting performance nor providing an implementation.
        
        Conceptually, \cite{lev} shares with \ftwoz \ the aim of committing to bits in a binary field, while proving MLE claims of those bits in some other structure $R$. Note that \cite{lev} is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, while \ftwoz \ can handle essentially all rings and fields $R$ of practical interest. Additionally it is not clear whether \cite{lev} can leverage any of our low-entropy oriented optimizations without substantially modifying the approach, incorporating some of our ideas. \albertimportant{Ask Lev if he has implemented his thing so we can compare. Also ask him if Binius64 is essentially following his method (Logup*, grand product, Frobenius)}

\subsection{\ftwoz\ in full generality}\label{s:to_general_bitz}


In general, \ftwoz can prove polynomial constraints over multiple rings $R_0$ (in the same instance) of the form $R[X]/(h(X))$, where $R$ is either the ring of integers $\ZZ$ or a finite field (not necessarily prime), and $h(X) \in R[X]$ is either the zero polynomial, or a monic non-constant polynomial. Examples of such rings $R_0$ include: the integers $\ZZ$, arbitrary finite fields, and lattice rings of the form $\FF_q[X]/(\Phi(X))$ for $\Phi(X)$ a cylcotomic polynomial and $q$ a prime power. More generally, $R_0$ can be  any two-generated ring of zero or prime characteristic.

 Concretely, we define a target relation, which we  call \emph{Universal Polynomial Satisfiability (UPESAT)}, as follows. Let $\codedim$ be a witness length and $\inplen$ a public input length. Let $R$ be either $\ZZ$ or a finite field. Let $\bm{R}=(R_i)_{i\in [\iota]}$ be a collection of rings of the form $R_i = R[X]/(h_i(X))$ for $h_i(X)\in R[X]$ either zero or a monic non-constant polynomial. For each $i\in [\iota]$, let $\pp^{(i)}$ be a vector of $\numrows$ polynomials
$$\mathbf{p}^{(i)} = \left(\left.\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)} \ \right| \ j\in[\numrows]\right) \in \left((\ring[X])[Y_1,\ldots, Y_{\inplen+\codedim}]\right)^{\numrows}$$ with coefficients in $R[X]$ and on $\inplen + \codedim$ variables $Y_1,\ldots, Y_{\inplen+\codedim}$. Given $a\in \ring[X]$ and $i\in [\iota]$, we say that \emph{$a=0$ holds in $R_i$} if the image of $a$ under the canonical projection $\ring[X]\to R_i$ is zero.  Finally, given a degree bound $\moduledegree$ and a bit-size bound, we let $R_{\leq B}^{<\moduledegree}[X]$ be the subset of $R[X]$ formed by polynomials of degree $<\moduledegree$ and coefficients in $R$ of bit-size $\leq B$ (see Step 1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} for a formalization of how we bit-decompose elements from $R$).

We define our target relation as
%
$$
%\UPESAT(\ringtwobounds{B}{\moduledegree}, \pp,\bm{R}, \cC, \pack) 
\UPESAT(R_{\leq B}^{<\moduledegree}[X], \pp,\bm{R}) 
= \left\{\left. 
\begin{aligned}
    \inp &= \xx \in \left(R_{\leq B}^{<\moduledegree}[X]\right)^{\inplen}\\
    \wit &=\ww \in \left(R_{\leq B}^{<\moduledegree}[X]\right)^\codedim
\end{aligned} 
\ \right| \ 
\begin{aligned}
    %& f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack \circ \picomfield(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
    &\mathbf{p}_{j}^{(i)}(\xx, \ww) = 0 \ \text{ in } R_i \ \text{ for all } j\in [\numrows], \ i \in [\iota]
\end{aligned}
\right\}
$$
%


\begin{remark}[Non prime characteristics or arithmetic modulo arbitrary integers]\label{r:non_prime_characteristics}
UPESAT does not allow to work with non-prime and non-zero characteristics $m$. I.e.\ it does not support, for example, $\ZZ/2^n\ZZ$ (i.e.\ arithmetic modulo $m=2^n$). Following \cite{zaratan,zinc}, we arithmetize such characteristics in $\ZZ$ or  $\ZZ[X]$ via the standard trick of adding a term of the form $+ m\cdot w$ to the constraints, for $w$ new witness elements. The correctness and soundness of this step follows from the fact that $a+b=c$ in $\ZZ/m\ZZ$ if and only if $a+b=c + m\cdot w$ in $\ZZ$ for some $w\in \ZZ$. Similar identities hold for  rings of non-prime and non-zero characteristic other than $\ZZ/m\ZZ$. See the introduction and technical overviews of \cite{zinc,limber} for more details.
\end{remark}

We note that UPESAT is a subset of the more general UCS relation from \cite{zincplus}. See \cref{r:pimsat_vs_ucs} for a comparison. \albert{Mention we could allow for different characteristics in the same relation?} 


\bigskip

Similarly as in \cref{s:to_f2_virtual}, \ftwoz\ can also handle a variation of $\UPESAT$ where $\bitstext(\ww)$ is a $\FF_2$-linear transformation of a vector of bits $\bff\in \comfield^{\codedim'}$.

We now explain how to modify the basic version of \ftwoz\ in \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} for the relation $\UPESAT$. There are two aspects that need modification:

\bigskip 

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item In Step 2 we need to design a method for transforming constraints in $R_i$ to constraints in a finite field $\FF$.
    \item In Step 3, $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}$ could be a proper extension of a prime field $\FF_p$. This occurs if for example $R_i = \FF_p$ for all $i\in [\iota]$ (i.e.\ all our constraints are over the prime field $\FF_p$), and $p$ is small (say, 32-64 bits), and so the PIOP in Step 3 needs to work on a field extension of $\FF_p$.
\end{enumerate}


\paragraph{Generalized Step 2.} Regarding Step 2, we discuss first the case that $\iota=1$ and $h_1=(0)$, so that all constraints are over $R[X]$, for $R$ the integers or a finite field, with witnesses belonging to $R_{\leq B}^{<\moduledegree}[X]$. In this case the verifier samples a map $\pi$ of one of the following forms:  If $R=\ZZ$ and $\moduledegree>0$, 
%
\begin{align*}
\pi: \ZZ[X]  &\to \FF_q, \qquad\qquad
g(X) \mapsto \pi_q(g)(\alpha)
\end{align*}
for a random prime $q$ and a random $\alpha\in \FF_q$, where $\pi_q$ denotes reduction modulo $q$.

If $R= \FF$ is a finite field of characteristic $q$, then  
\begin{align*}
\pi: \FF[X]  &\to \FF', \qquad\qquad
g(X) \mapsto g(\alpha)
\end{align*}
for $\FF'$ possibly a field extension of $\FF$ (so that $|\FF'|=O(2^\lambda)$) and $\alpha\in \FF'$.   \albert{Say a few words of why this is sound (once I've written the soundness intuition for the basic protocol}




The  general case where not all ideals $(h_i(X))$ are  zero is dealt  in the same way as in Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}. We omit the details here for the sake of brevity. We refer to \cref{s:iop_upesat} (Items 3 and 4 of Round 1, and Round 2, of \cref{c:iop_pimsat}) or to  the technical overview of \cite{zincplus} \albert{Should I describe this here?}. 




\paragraph{The case when $\FFext$ is a proper extension} Next we discuss the scenario where $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}$ is a proper field extension at the end of Step 3 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. In this case, Step 4 proceeds in the same way as in the normal case. At the end of Step 4 prover and verifier are left with the claim
%
$$
\langle \uu, \pi(\bitstext(\ww))\rangle = \mu \qquad \text{in } \FFext,
$$
for some $\uu\in \FFext^{\codedim}$ and $\mu\in \FFext$.  

After Step 4, Step 5.0 triggers \albertimportant{Again, in the main body this step triggers after step 5.1 and is slightly different}. Since $\FFext$ is a finite field, it is of the form $\FFext=\ZZ[X]/(q,f(X))$ for an irreducible polynomial $f(X)$ over $\FF_q[X]$.  Let $\picanon: \ZZ[X] \to \FFext$ be the canonical homomorphism sending $g(X)\in \ZZ[X]$ to its reduction modulo $q$ and modulo $f(X)$. 

The prover sends an integer polynomial $\mu'\in \ZZ[X]$ such that
    %
    $$\langle  \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bitstext(\uu)\rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ[X],$$    
    The verifier checks that $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, and responds with a random prime $q'$ such that $q'< \fieldboundsizeexponent$  and with a random prime field element $\alpha\in \FF_{q'}$. Then prover and verifier proceed to Step 5.1 after updating $\FFext \gets \FF_{q'}$, $\pi\gets \pi_{q',\alpha}$, $\mu \gets \pi_{q',\alpha}(\mu')\in \FF_{q'}$, and $$\uu\gets \pi_{q',\alpha}\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pi_{q',\alpha}\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}),$$
    where $\pi_{q',\alpha}: \ZZ[X] \to \FF_{q'}$ is defined as $$\pi_{q',\alpha}(g(X)) = \pi_{q'}(g)(\alpha) \in \FF_{q'}.$$
\albert{Again, say a few words of why this is sound (once I've written the soundness intuition for the basic protocol}

One can, instead of sampling morphisms of the form $\pi_{q',\alpha}$, sample other types of $\ZZ$-module homomorphisms  $\pi':\ZZ^{\mathsf{deg}(\FFext)}[X]$ to a prime field.  See \cref{r:otheSodule_morphisms}.  This can modestly improve the soundness errors at the expense of increasing the communication complexity. \albert{It may be a good idea to present the protocols as parametrized by a family of module homomorphisms where we sample from. Right now the protocols have the fixed family above. Let's see if there's time}

For example, in our full proving system in \cref{c:iop_pimsat}, constraints can be (among others) over $\ZZ$ or $\ZZ[X]$, then they are projected to a random prime field $\FF_q$, a PIOP is ran on them, and an MLE claim over $\FF_p$ is produced. \section{Preliminaries}

\paragraph{General notation} Given an integer $n\geq 1$, we write $[n]$ to denote the set $\{1,\ldots, n\}$. Given integers $m\leq n$, we let $\range{m}{n}$ denote the set of integers $\{m, m+1,. \ldots, n\}$.

We use $\circ$ to denote composition of maps, so that for maps $f: S_1\to S_2$ and $g:S_2\to S_3$, we have $f\circ g: S_1 \to S_3$ defined as $f\circ g(x) = f(g(x))$. 

Any map $f:S_1 \to S_2$ applies  entry-wise to vectors from $S_1^n$, for any $n$, and similarly for matrices. A map $f$ also applies coefficieny-wise to polynomials with coefficients in $S_1$. A multiplication of a vector  or a matrix by a scalar means that we multiply each entry of the vector or matrix by that scalar. 

We look at vectors both as row vectors and column vectors. When performing matrix-vector multiplications, it will always be clear which one we intend. Given a ring $R$, a  matrix $M\in R^{n\times m}$ and  vectors $\uu\in R^m$, $\vv\in R^n$, one has
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:matrix_transpose_inner_product}
\langle M\cdot \uu, \vv \rangle = \langle \uu, M^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv\rangle,
\end{equation}
%
where $M^{\mathsf{T}}$ denotes transposition and $\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle$ denotes inner product.

\subsection{Algebra}\label{s:prelims_algebra}
We write $\FF_q$ for the finite field with $q$ elements (where $q$ is a prime or a prime power), $\QQ$ for the field of rational numbers, and $\ZZ$ for the ring of integers. For an integer $n\geq 1$, $\ZZ_n$ denotes the ring of integers modulo $n$, with the convention $\ZZ_0\defeq \ZZ$. When $n$ is prime, $\ZZ_n=\FF_n$. We let $$\pi_n :\ZZ\to \ZZ_n$$ denote reduction modulo $n$. We fix $\pi_n^{-1} : \ZZ_n \to \range{0}{n-1}\subseteq \ZZ$ map each element $a$ in $\ZZ_n$ to the integer in the range  $\range{0}{n-1}$ representing $a$. 



All rings in this work are associative, commutative, have a multiplicative identity. Given a ring $R$, we write $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ for the ring of polynomials with coefficients in $R$ and variables $X_1, \ldots, X_n$ (not necessarily multilinear). 

 
 All rings in this work are \emph{finitely generated} as rings. Equivalently, every ring $R$ in this work is isomorphic to a quotient $$R=\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k]/I$$ of a polynomial ring over $\ZZ$ by an ideal $I$. This condition is satisfied by, for example:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item All finite fields and the rings $\ZZ_n$; 
\item $\ZZ$, and the polynomial rings $\ZZ[X]$ and $\FF_q[X]$
\item Arbitrary quotients of the previous rings, such as $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for an arbitrary polynomial $h(X)$ and an arbitrary integer $n\geq 0$, which includes  lattice rings. 
\end{enumerate}
It is not satisfied by, for example, $\QQ$ and $\QQ[X]$, which are not finitely generated as rings. Some constructions in this work  impose further requirements on the rings they use. We state these when  needed.



 The \emph{characteristic} of a ring $\ring$, denoted $\Char(R)$, is the smallest $c\geq 1$, if it exists, such that $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 = 0$ in $\ring$. Otherwise, if $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 \neq 0$ for all $c$, the characteristic is zero.
 By $\range{0}{n}_\ring$ we denote all elements in $\ring$ of the form $1 + \overset{i}{\ldots} + 1$ for $i=0,\ldots, n$.
 
 %An ideal $I$ of a ring $R$ generated by a set $S\subseteq R$ is denoted $(S)$.


\albertimportant{Not used?} Given a  ring $R$, $n$ variables $X_1, \ldots, X_n$, and a bit-size bound $B\geq 1$, we define 
%
$$
R_{\leq B}[X_1, \ldots, X_n]
$$
%
as the subset of $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ formed by polynomials all whose coefficients are in the set $\range{0}{2^B-1}_R$.  


\bigskip

%\albertimportant{Not sure we want to keep the module stuff} Given a ring homomorphism $\psi: \ring_1\to \ring_2$, a $\ring_1$-module $M_{\ring_1}$, and a $\ring_2$-module, a $\psi$-module homomorphism between $M_{\ring_1}$ and $M_{\ring_2}$ is a map $\phi$ such that $\phi(\alpha\cdot a + \beta \cdot b) = \psi(\alpha)\cdot \phi(a) + \psi(\beta) \cdot \phi(b)$ for all $\alpha,\beta\in \ring_1$ and $a,b\in M_{\ring_1}$.

   
%Given a base $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_\moduledegree$ for $M_\ring$, we let $\moduleonebound$ be the subset of $M_\ring$ with elements whose coordinates belong to $\ringbitbound{B}$ in this base.







%\albertimportant{Let's remove this. The notation above should be enough} Given a  finite field $\FF$ of characteristic $q$, let $a(X)\in \ZZ[X]$ be an irreducible polynomial over $\FF_q$ so that $\FF = \ZZ[X]/(q,a(X))$ \albert{consider isomorphism, or assume all finite fields are represented like this}. We let $$\pi_{\FF}: \ZZ[X] \to \FF$$ be the  canonical homomorphism from $\ZZ[X]$ to $\FF$ obtained by reducing modulo the ideal $(q,a(X))$, i.e.\ by reducing coefficients modulo $q$ and then reducing the resulting polynomial modulo $a(X)$.

\albertimportant{Needed?} A subset $S\subseteq R$ of a  ring $R$ is called \emph{exceptional} if for all two distinct $a,b\in R$, the element $a-b$ has a multiplicative inverse in $R$.
\begin{lemma}[Generalized Alon--F{\"u}redi Theorem~\cite{BCPS18}]
\label{lem:sz_exceptional_sets}
Let $R$ be a ring and let $S\subseteq R$ be a finite exceptional subset of $R$. Let $f(\YY)\in R[\YY]$ be a nonzero multilinear polynomial in $\mu$ variables. Then
$\Pr_{\rr\getsr S^\mu}[f(\rr)=0] \le \mu/|S|$.
\end{lemma}

\subsection{Linear codes and relations}\label{s:prelims_codes}

\paragraph{Linear codes}

Let $\cC \subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code over $\FF$ of dimension $\codedim$ and blocklength $\codelength$. For $h\geq 1$, the \emph{$h$-interleaving} of $\cC$ is the code $\cC^{\equiv h} \subseteq \left(\FF^{h}\right)^{\codelength}$ of dimension $\codedim\cdot h$ and blocklength $\codelength\cdot h$, whose codewords have the form $$\left((c_{i1},\ldots, c_{ih})\right)_{i\in [\codelength]} \qquad \text{for } (c_{1j},\ldots, c_{\codelength j})\in \cC \qquad \text{for all } j\in [h].$$

Given two words $w_1, w_2$ of length $n$, the relative Hamming distance between $w_1$ and $w_2$ is denoted 
%
$$\hamming(w_1,w_2).$$
The \emph{relative minimum distance} of $\cC$, denoted $\hamming(\cC)$, is the minimum of $\hamming(c_1,c_2)$ over distinct codewords $c_1,c_2\in \cC$. Given $\delta\in (0,1)$ and an integer $L\geq 1$, we say $\cC$ is \emph{$(\delta, L)$-list-decodable} if for every word $w\in \FF^{\codelength}$ there are at most $L$ codewords $c\in \cC$ with $\hamming(w,c)<\delta$. Every code is $(\delta,1)$-list-decodable for $\delta\leq \hamming(\cC)/2$.  The value $\hamming(\cC)/2$ is called the \emph{unique decoding radius} of $\cC$.

\paragraph{Relations}
A \emph{relation} $\Relation$ consists of triples  $(\inp,\oracle;\wit)$, where $\inp$ is the explicit instance, $\oracle$ the implicit instance or oracle, and $\wit$ the witness.  The corresponding language  is 
\(
  \LANG(\Relation)\ \defeq\ \bigl\{(\inp,\oracle)\ \bigm|\ \exists\,\wit \text{ such that } (\inp. \oracle,\wit)\in\Relation\bigr\}.
\)

\paragraph{Polynomial Equation Satisfiability (PESAT) over finite fields} We recall a relation from \cite{linear-accumulation} \albert{Linear time accumulation paper, or earlier?} over finite fields, which captures popular constraints such as R1CS, CCS, AIR (cf.\ \cite{linear-accumulation}), and lookup and permutation constraints (cf.\ \cite{zinc}).

Fix a finite field $\FF$, a witness length $\codedim$, an input length $\inplen$, a number of rows $n$, a collection of $n$ polynomials $\pp=(\mathbf{p}_{1}, \ldots, \pp_\numrows)$ on $\inplen+\codedim$ variables, for each $j\in [\numrows]$. 

\begin{definition}\label{d:pesat} The PESAT relation with respect to these parameters is defined as
$$
\PESAT(\FF,\pp) = \left\{\left. 
\begin{aligned}
    \inp &= \xx \in \FF^{\inplen},\\
    \oracle &= \bot ,\\
    \wit &=\bff \in \FF^\codedim
\end{aligned} 
\ \right| \ 
\begin{aligned}
    \mathbf{p}_{i}(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows].
\end{aligned}
\right\}
$$
\end{definition}
%
Let $\cC\subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code of dimension $\codedim'$ and blocklength $\codelength$ over $\FF$.    Let $W\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$ be a subset and let $T:W \to \FF^{\codedim'}$ be an arbitrary map (looking ahead, for us $T$ will be a packing map that packs elements from $\FF_2$ into elements of a larger binary field, so that $W=\FF_2^{\codedim}\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$).  We define the \emph{Encoded PESAT  relation} as: %\albert{No reason to expose the interleaving this early}
%
$$
\PESAT(\FF, \pp, \cC, T) \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=\xx\in \FF^\inplen, \\&\oracle = f \in \FF^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in W\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}
&\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)) = f\\ \wedge \  &\pp_i(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows]\end{aligned}\right\}
$$
We will drop the reference to $T$ whenever it is understood from the context. When $\numrows=1$ and $\xx$ are such that $\xx=(\vv,\mu)\in \FF^{\codedim}\times \FF$; $\pp_1(\xx,\ff)= \langle \ff, \vv\rangle -\mu$ (and $\codedim=\inplen$), the relation $\PESAT(\FF,\pp, \cC, T)$ is called a \emph{constrained code} \cite{WHIR?} \albert{WHIR?}.  We denote it $$\LIN(\FF,\cC,T).$$
%
\begin{definition}[Relaxation of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$]Given $\delta \in (0,1)$, the \emph{relaxation} of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, denoted
%
$$
\PESAT^\delta, \qquad \qquad \LIN^{\delta}
$$
%
are the relations defined exactly as $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, respectively, except that the constraint $\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff))=f$ is replaced by
%
$$
\hamming(\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)), f) < \delta.
$$
\end{definition}


\subsection{Interactive Oracle Reductions (IOR) of proximity}\label{s:IORs}

A public-coin \emph{Interactive Oracle Reduction (IOR) of proximity} $\Pi=(\prover,\verifier)$ from a relation $\Relation$ to a relation $\Relation'$ is specified as follows. The prover $\prover$ receives $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in \Relation$. The verifier $\verifier$ receives $\inp$ and query access to $\oracle$. They interact for $k$ rounds. In round $j\in[k]$, the prover sends an oracle $\provermsg_j$ and the verifier sends a uniformly random message $\verifiermsg_j\in\{0,1\}^{r_j}$. After the $k$ rounds, the verifier makes  queries to $\oracle$ and to $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$. Then:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $\verifier$ either rejects or outputs a new  pair $(\inp', \oracle')$, where $\oracle'$ is specified implicitly in terms of the oracles $\oracle$ and $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$.
  \item $\prover$ outputs a witness $\wit'$ such that $(\inp', \oracle', \wit')\in \Relation'$.
\end{itemize}
We write $ \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)$ for the interaction of prover and verifier during the IOR.

\begin{definition}[Completeness]
\label{def:ior-completeness}
$\Pi$ has \emph{(perfect) completeness}  if for every $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in\Relation$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_1,\dots,\verifiermsg_k}\bigl[\,(\inp',\oracle';\wit')\in \Relation'\, \mid \, (\inp',\oracle';\wit') \gets \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)\bigr]=1.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    An IOP for a relation $\Relation$ is an IOR from $\Relation$ to the trivial relation $\Relation_{\mathsf{triv}}=\{(\inp=\bot,\oracle=\bot, \wit=\bot)\}$.
\end{remark}

We proceed to define round-by-round knowledge soundness of an IOR, as presented in \cite{TCC:BCFW25}.



\begin{definition}[Knowledge state function with relaxation]
\label{def:knowledge-state-function}
Let $\Pi = (\prover, \verifier)$ be an IOR from a relation $\Relation$ to $\Relation'$. A \emph{knowledge state function with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})$} for $\Pi$ is a (possibly inefficient) function $\kstate$ that, on input  $(\inp, \oracle)$, interaction transcript $\tr$, and knowledge state witness $\kswit$, outputs a bit and has the following properties.
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Empty transcript.} If $\tr = \emptyset$ is the empty transcript, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $(\inp, \oracle; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation}$.
  \item \textbf{Prover moves.} If $\tr$ is a transcript where the prover is about to move and $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 0$, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr \Vert \provermsg, \kswit) = 0$ for every prover message $\provermsg$.
  \item \textbf{Full transcript.} If $\tr = (\provermsg_1, \verifiermsg_1, \ldots, \provermsg_k, \verifiermsg_k)$ is a full transcript, then $\kstate(\inp, \oracle, \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $\verifier$ on $\tr$ outputs $(\inp', \oracle')$ such that $(\inp', \oracle'; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation'}$.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Round-by-round knowledge soundness]
\label{def:rbr-knowledge-new}
A $k$-round IOR $\Pi$ has \emph{round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation} with errors $(\knowledgeerror_1,\ldots,\knowledgeerror_k)$ if there exists a knowledge state function $\kstate$ as in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} and a deterministic extractor $\rbrextractor$ such that for every $(\inp,\oracle)$, every $j\in[k]$, and every transcript $\tr$ where the verifier is about to send $\verifiermsg_j$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_j}\!\left[
  \exists\,\kswit \ \text{such that} \ \begin{array}{l}
  \wedge\ \kstate\bigl(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\rbrextractor(\inp,\oracle,\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)\bigr)=0\\
  \wedge\ \kstate(\inp,\oracle,\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)=1
  \end{array}
 \right]
  \ \le\ \knowledgeerror_j(\inp,\oracle).
\]
If $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})=(\Relation, \Relation')$ then we say the round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi$ is \emph{without relaxation}.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Composition of IORs]\label{r:ior_compositoin}
\albert{Turn into a theorem/lemma. What can I cite for this? TensorSwitch, Arc, or ZOOK?} IORs can be composed together and inherit completeness and round-by-round knowledge soundness errors. More precisely, let $\Pi_1,\Pi_1$ be perfectly complete IORs from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_2$  and $\Relation_3$, respectively, with round-by-round knowledge errors  with relaxation  $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_2})$ and $(\widetilde{\Relation_2},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$, respectively; and with errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots, \kappa_{1k_1}$ and $\kappa_{21}, \ldots, \kappa_{2k_2}$, respectively. Consider  the IOR $\Pi'$ from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_3$ consisting of applying IOR $\Pi_1$ first, and then $\Pi_2$ taking as input for $\Pi_2$ the output of $\Pi_1$.   Then $\Pi'$ is a perfectly complete $(k_1+k_2)$-round IOR from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_3$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness  with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$ and errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots, \kappa_{1k_1}, \kappa_{21}, \ldots, \kappa_{2k_2}$.
%
\end{remark}


\paragraph{Grinding}\albert{Review}
 Grinding \cite{?} is a technique where, given am IOP $\Pi$ for a relation $\Relation$ with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with round-by-round knowledge errors $\kappa_1,\ldots, \kappa_k$,  produces a new IOP $\Pi'$ for $\Relation$ with   round-by-round knowledge with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with errors $$2^{-\alpha_1}\cdot \kappa_1,\ldots, 2^{-\alpha_k}\cdot \kappa_k.$$ The new IOP $\Pi'$ works exactly as $\Pi$, except that, at each round $i\in [t]$, the verifier samples a random string of $\alpha_i$ bits, called \emph{grinding string}, which sends alongside its message, and rejects unless all bits are $0$. 

 Clearly $\Pi'$ is not  necessarily complete, even if $\Pi$ is. The goal is to use the BCS compilation of IOP's, and not the IOP's themselves (i.e.\ the compilation where oracles are replaces by Merkle roots and where interactiveness is removed through the Fiat-Shamir transform) To this end, one chooses $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_k$ small enough that, the prover of the BCS transform of $\Pi'$ can \emph{grind} transcripts until all of the grinding strings in the transcript are the zero string. This results in the prover having to compute  per round $i\in [t]$, an average of $2^{\alpha_i}$  hashes of its $i$-th message followed by a small string of bits (in practice, one has the prover send a small string of bits next to its message, so that during grinding, it does not need to produce fresh Merkle roots at each grinding iteration). In \cref{tab:grinding-cost}, we provide example grinding times for different values of $\alpha_i$.
 
 The round-by-round knowledge errors of $\Pi'$ are obtained by defining a state function which is exactly as the state function of $\Pi$, except that the function outputs zero if some of the aforementioned strings of bits are not the zero string. The extractor for $\Pi'$ is defined as the extractor for $\Pi$, ignoring the additional strings of bits in the transcript.
